and stored in one memory block of the RFID tag. Along with the LPN, the
tag stores the Carrier ID, Destination, Date, Time, Passenger Name,
Passenger Mobile, CRC, and other information.
The Control Center harmonizes groups of readers and ﬁlters raw
data/events to eliminate redundancies. Web Services provide service
interfaces and APIs to human interaction applications. In Distributed
Monitors, query results may be shown on a GIS map, and wireless mobile
terminals can alert passengers when baggage is successfully loaded.
IV.B: Framework and Components
The RFID tag is UHF (862–900 MHz) with a unique identiﬁcation number,
corresponding to the 850–950 MHz frequency spectrum set by IATA. The
reader sends data over the network to a Document Management System
(DMS) to monitor, manage, and store documents while reducing paper
usage.
IV.C: Integrated Structure
The developed integrated system consists of a web server and a database
server. The Point-of-Sale (POS) system generates a new tag identiﬁer for
the RFID tag, transmited to the Access database. The Access ﬁle is
instantly exported to a Google Fusion Table, keeping track of baggage
tracking history and updating the Mobile App.
IV.D: Implementation
The program is installed at BCIA Terminal 2 in Beijing. One RFID EPC C1 G2
UHF tag from 'UPM Raﬂatac' is sealed in each baggage strip. Readers with
three antennae called 'Intermec IF5' are set in tracking zones of the T2 BHS
area. TCP/IP Ethernet connects all readers to the control center. SMS
gateways send conﬁrmation messages to travelers regarding successful
baggage sorting.

VI. Challenges
System adoption requires collaboration across baggage makers, retail
stores, airports, airlines, hotels, and customs. Frontline worker training,
vector data tracking limitations, security, and data protection represent key
hurdles.
VI.A: Infrastructure
Building a UHF RFID infrastructure across the top 80 airports is critical to
cover up to 80% of all baggage mishandling. Solutions like ONS (Object
Naming Service) leveraging DNS should be implemented to handle large-
scale tracking.

VII.B: Eﬃciency Improvements
RFID tag reading is unaﬀected by dirt, partial folding, or concealment,
accelerating ULD loading lists and streamlining operations.
VII.C: Potential Worthiness
Passenger claims are estimated to decrease by 5.7 million following RFID
implementation, alongside enabling new value-added services and data
mining.
VIII. Conclusion
The RFID technology provides real-time visibility and strengthens
automated BHS solutions. Key takeaways include:
I. RFID outperforms barcodes with an average read rate of 96.86
percent versus 75%–85% for barcodes.
II. Initial installation costs should be borne by airports, while operating
costs are split proportionally between airports and airlines.
III. Barcode-based and RFID-based systems will coexist until global
network infrastructure is fully established.

A research essay concerning Electronic
Baggage Tags
By: Edris Nazparvar
Technology should be used to aid us in our day-to-day life This essay is a
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 272 of 346

Technology should be used to aid us in our day to day life. This essay is a
collection of Articles concerning Electronic Baggage Tags, an electronic
device made for tracking and identifying Baggage. There are some key
questions concerning mentioned subject that this essay will try to provide
answers for them:
What is an Electronic Baggage Tag?
What are the beneﬁts of using it instead of a traditional paper-based
Baggage tag?
What can be the diﬃculties of using it?
What are the technological speciﬁcations?
I. Abstract
The aviation sector is one of the industries where Radio Frequency
Identiﬁcation (RFID) and the Internet of Things (IoT) have a lot of promise.
Mislaid baggage, lost baggage, and damage to valuables are the most
typical ﬂaws in the aviation industry's baggage handling. With over two
billion people traveling by air each year, the aviation industry is under
pressure to enhance baggage monitoring and minimize the use of one-
time sticky baggage paper tags.
RFID stands for radio frequency identiﬁcation and is a kind of automated
identiﬁcation. To cover all elements of airline baggage monitoring,
sortation, and reconciliation, several distinct types of RFID prototype
systems are currently being developed. RFID is not only a viable, unique,
and cost-eﬀective choice for everyday object identiﬁcation, but it is also
seen as a critical instrument for providing traceable visibility across the
aviation supply chain's many phases.
RFID scanners at check-out facilities allow for step-by-step baggage
tracking, preventing baggage loss. The baggage's real-time location is
recorded and saved in the cloud via IoT, and the travelers' unique ID may be
accessed anywhere and whenever they need it. At check-out counters, the
same ID can be used to collect the bag. The technology supplied saves
time, assures baggage security, and is cost-eﬀective, resulting in customer
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 273 of 346

happiness.
II. Introduction
In 2009, global airlines lost 25 million pieces of passenger baggage [1]. On
foreign ﬂights in 2011, 26 million pieces of checked baggage were missing
[2]. 25.8 million pieces of baggage were mishandled in 2012, with 85
percent being delayed and 15% being damaged or lost [3].
Some baggage is located following track and trace within a few hours,
while others are declared permanently lost. Airports and airlines expend
enormous energy to seek down and return lost baggage to passengers, at
tremendous expense; Swedberg [6] estimated an annual cost of US$750
million to track down and restore lost baggage to its owner through airline,
airport, and courier.
The IATA RP1740c protocol is the one that is used to recognize RFID
baggage tags. The RFID tags are used to enhance the ability for baggage
tracking, dispatching and conveyance to improve the management
eﬃciency and the users’ satisfaction.
The fundamental RFID system is composed of three components:
Transponder/Tag
Reader
Backend Application
RFID technology has been investigated and deployed in a number of
airports across the world, including Heathrow Airport in London, Kansai
International Airport in Japan, and Hong Kong International Airport in Hong
Kong.
III. Background
III.A: Standard
There are two important standards that will play signiﬁcant roles in the
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 274 of 346

supply chain:
The ISO 18000 serial standard developed by the International
Standards Organization (ISO)
The EPC standard advanced by EPCglobal
The EPC standard addresses air interfaces, the format for product
identiﬁcation data contained in RFID tags, middleware [74], and databases
that hold tag data. The ISO 18000-6C standard was met by the EPC Class -1
Generation 2 UHF Speciﬁcation provided by EPCglobal. It speciﬁes the
speciﬁcations for a radio-frequency identiﬁcation system operating in the
860MHz to 960MHz frequency band with passive-back scater and
Interrogator talk ﬁrst. This frequency range avoids regional variances in
UHF radio spectrum allotment for RFID, such as in North America (902 to
928MHz), Europe (865.6 to 867.6MHz), and Japan (from 953 to 954MHz)
[62].
An interrogator (also known as a reader) is in charge of reading data from a
tag by sending it a continuous-wave RF signal. The tag replies by modifying
its antenna's reﬂection coeﬃcient, backscatering an information signal to
the interrogator. The RF signal provides both information and working
energy to the tag, which is passive [48]. Because UHF works well in dry,
non-metallic setings, it is recommended that the aircraf baggage tag
operate in the UHF frequency range.
The UHF RFID tag could be reprogrammed and reused. By establishing self-
service kiosks, operating expenses might be minimized.
With the use of RFID in baggage tracking, smartphone apps might be used
to show and interact with human and tracking data. Users may track and
trace the whereabouts of their baggage using their mobile devices at any
time and from any location. The smartphone sofware might also show real-
time ﬂight information and help with the check-in procedure. It might also
serve as a communication hub between the traveler and the airline prior to
departure. Airline shopping combined with commercial transactions
conducted through mobile applications might result in added value and
client retention.
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 275 of 346

III.B: IATA
In terms of baggage handling regulations, the IATA, in collaboration with
airline companies, airports, and RFID vendors, addresses the open protocol
known as RP1740c [46][54]. It speciﬁes that the air interface used is ISO
18000-6C, and that the application protocol is ISO/IEC 15961 & 15962, which
describe data compression for storage and the instructions that may be
used to interact with the tag, respectively.

III.C: Airports & Airlines
Many airlines and airports, such as Hong Kong International Airport, Delta
Airlines, McCarran International Airport, British Airways/Heathrow Airport,
Singapore Airlines, and Narita International Airport, have been working on
the conversion from traditional barcode-based baggage handling to RFID.
There are two types of RFID-based BHS adoption mechanisms. In the initial
application form, low-cost tags (about $0.25 per tag) are used to index a
single piece of baggage, similar to how one plate for one automobile is
utilized. Only when the backdrop has been established can baggage
information such as the owner, origin, and destination be enquired about.
The advantage of this manner is that the one-time tag is inexpensive, and
the reader sofware is simple to implement, but the back infrastructure is
sophisticated, and oﬀ-line baggage information disposal is not supported.
In the second case, more expensive tags with more memory are employed
to keep precise information about the baggage, and the status of the
baggage may be tracked at every stage once it reaches the airport. The
front-end reader is more diﬃcult to install, but the demand on the back-
end system is reduced, and oﬀ-line inquiry by portable reader or otherwise

is supported. In this mode, the tag's cost is higher, allowing it to be
recycled.
IV. Application
IV.A: Design
Figure 0 depicts the experimental setup. The UHF tag is sealed inside the
barcode-printed baggage strip, and dual mode baggage handling
operations are available. With minimal work and cost, RFID can
complement the industry's present barcode-based automated handling
solutions. One RFID Tracking Node consists of a TCP/IP-enabled reader and
two or three ﬁxed antennas that emit RF signals in several directions.
(See Figure 0: Topology of the RFID-Based Traceable Air Baggage Handling
System)
Each node is placed at a crucial location in the handling area to scan and
update information on the baggage moving through. The physical
foundation tier of the system is made up of all of the baggage strips with
tags and the tracking nodes. When a departing passenger checks in, the
license plate number is not only writen in human-readable barcode format
on the strip, but it is also translated and saved in one memory block of the
RFID tag sealed in the strip.
Along with the LPN, the tag stores the Carrier ID, Destination, Date, Time,
Passenger Name, Passenger Mobile, CRC, and other information. The
Control Center, which includes modules like the reader manager and event
manager, is in charge of harmonizing huge groups of readers, even if they
are dissimilar. The raw data or events connected to the tags must be
ﬁltered, aggregated, and cleaned to eliminate redundancies since the
original data requested from RFID monitoring nodes is too vast.
Duplicate entries resulting from several readings of the same bag at the
same place, for example, are combined. Afer ﬁltering and smoothing, the
tag reading data in the database can be compressed and processed in a
variety of ways. When using these additional procedures, there are various
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 278 of 346

considerations to consider. For example, when the system scale is inside
one terminal of the airport, these operations can be avoided. Web Services
are used by the Information Server to provide service interfaces and APIs
to human interaction applications.
These given services place a premium on the question of baggage
placement. In Distributed Monitors, the query results may be shown on a
GIS map. Furthermore, when the correct scenario is identiﬁed by RFID
Tracking Node, various services may be provided to users' wireless mobile
terminals, such as alerting the passenger that his baggage has been
successfully loaded on the airplane.
On the GUI of distributed monitors, we track all local and transfer baggage
from customer check-in through placement into containers for aircraf
using meaning information abstracted from the primitive data. Some
researchers created additional devices, such as a bracelet [23] and a
smartwatch [41], to help with baggage monitoring and receiving
information. Instead of one-time RFID baggage tags, extra accessories, and
web-based tracking, the suggested RFID cyber-physical system's
architecture includes an integrated reusable baggage tag that is linked to
the baggage. With the help of mobile application devices, the location of
the baggage may be tracked at any time.
IV.B: Framework and Components
The RFID tag is UHF (862–900 MHz) and has a unique identiﬁcation number.
This corresponds to the 850–950 MHz frequency spectrum set by the
International Air Transport Association (IATA) for RFID baggage tags. The
RFID antenna is utilized to identify the tag as well as receive the tag's
broadcast signal. The data is sent over the network by the reader to a DMS.
The use of a document management system (DMS) to monitor, manage,
and store documents reduces the amount of paper needed.
The data is subsequently processed by the DMS and sent to the mobile
app. The data obtained from the airline, airport terminal, and mobile app
application is also integrated into the DMS. The mobile application is
loaded on a smartphone mobile device and allows tourists to follow their
baggage using the app's mobile tracking features.

10/19/26, 1:59 AM

IV.C: Integrated Structure
The developed integrated system consists of a web server and a database
server. The web server covers the operation of the webpage and the
communication between the external workstations and internal servers.
The communication includes queries and commands transmited through
the web server. The database server connects the RFID reader, stores the
data, and manages the information of the RFID tag.
Figure 1 depicts the architecture of the integrated system as well as the
communication protocol. The reader transmits signals to the tag and
receives the answers using two-way radio transmiter-receivers (Figure 2).
The Point-of-Sale (POS) system generates a new tag identiﬁer for the RFID
tag, which is subsequently transmited to the Access database. Additional
consumer personal information might be simply entered into the POS or
database.
The Access ﬁle is instantly exported to a Google Fusion Table, which keeps
track of the most recent and previous baggage tracking information. The
information is updated on the Mobile App, allowing users to track their
bags and look for relevant baggage and ﬂight schedule information.
IV.D: Implementation
The program is installed at BCIA Terminal 2, one of China's largest
passenger terminals, which houses virtually all of the airlines' domestic and
international ﬂights departing from Beijing. One RFID EPC C1 G2 UHF tag
from 'UPM Raﬂatac' [70] is sealed in each baggage strip (Figure 3-a).
In this scenario, one tag corresponds to a piece of baggage and a
passenger with his E-TKT. These Gen 2 tags are ISO 18000-6C compliant
and are more accessible. We set readers with three antennae called
'Intermec IF5' [58] (Figure 4) in the tracking zones of the T2 BHS area, such
as behind the security check area, the end of the check-in island, baggage
conveyor belt, manual recode station, and sorting exit for each aircraf.
TCP/IP Ethernet connects all of the readers to the control center. The tag
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 280 of 346

may be read at a distance by any of the antennas using the command
packet (Figure 3-b) transmited by the reader, and many bags can be read
at the same time. The protocol between the reader and the tag is depicted
by the command request/response format. Where the 'Flags' designate the
tag query mode as select, inventory, or loop; the 'TagType' describes the
object tag type as EPC C1 Gen2, ISO 18000-C, or auto-detect; the 'Address'
and 'BlockNo' specify the memory bank for reading or writing the tag; and
the 'CRC' is the calculated CCITT-16 cyclic redundancy check.
We put a single LED screen above each conveyor exit that is pre-
programmed for one speciﬁc planned ﬂight. The quantity of sorted and
sorting baggage for this ﬂight is displayed on the screen. The number of
pieces of baggage that were properly sorted and those that were sent to
the error sorting conveyor exit are displayed on the distributed monitor.
The GPRS Web gateway oﬀered by 'China Mobile' is installed in the control
center, as SMS has grown in popularity as a means for mobile data
transmission. In ISendSMS.jsp, we call the sendSMS function to send a
conﬁrmation message indicating if the baggage has been successfully
sorted before the passenger boards the plane. The use of SMS will provide
travelers with real-time feedback and increase passenger happiness.

hostile physical environment. We evaluate the read rate of the departing
baggage on the assumption that RFID is introduced for one speciﬁc
scheduled aircraf of AIR CHINA (CA) within two months (from May 16,
2006 to June 19, 2006). Here, the read rate is deﬁned as follows:
On the distributed monitors' GUI, the number of appropriately handled
pieces of bags that transit the accurate conveyor exit point automatically
increases for the trial planned ﬂight. All misread baggage is moved to the
designated 'trash' conveyor exit, where it will be scanned with a hand-held

According to the results shown in Figure 5, the average of varying baggage
read rates is 96.86 percent, which is a signiﬁcant improvement over a
barcode-only system (according to the IATA, barcode baggage tag read
rates average 85 percent, though other industry experts put that average
at 75 percent) [61], [63]. Furthermore, the increased visibility of baggage
precise tracking on dispersed monitors increases the industrial ﬁeld
worker's process management ability and helps to boost baggage security.
V. IoT — Database Management System, Fusion
Table and Mobile Tracking
Because of its interoperability and connectivity with other databases, user-
friendly programming interfaces, and widespread use in the industry, the
DMS is built using Microsof Access. With ﬂight information, baggage tag
personal information, and other data maintained in an organized manner,
Access allows for eﬃcient data storage and retrieval. For data retrieval and
record transactions, Access provides queries and form methods. Access
also allows you to export data to Google Fusion Table for further data
processing in mobile apps. Tag information, customers table, ﬂight
information table, stocks table, retail store table, baggage history, baggage
location, hotel table, and airline table are all established in the DMS. The
majority of the tables are linked together using main and foreign keys.
The relationship begins when a retail store sells baggage with an RFID tag,
which is reﬂected in the POS, and ends when a traveler brings the baggage
to the airport during a ﬂying journey. As an item of baggage, the
connection between the consumer and the baggage is one-to-many.
Figure 6 depicts the interaction between tag information, retail store for
tag, customer information, baggage location, hotel, and airline. Aside from
the nine tables, the data structure contains ﬁve forms for customer, hotel,
and tag information.
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 282 of 346

g
The baggage tag identiﬁcation number, time, and matching location are
shown in the baggage tag history table of access to allow tourists to follow
their baggage using mobile devices. For example, on August 18, 2022, at
20:30, a tag with the identifying number '000000000176' will be shown on
the traveler's mobile device.
Ten milestones are identiﬁed in the baggage process ﬂow with RFID
antenna set up as check points to identify the important milestones for
baggage tracking and tracing (Figure 7). Afer being purchased in a retail
store, the tag is assigned the default status of ‘ready to use,’ which is
updated in the POS system. When the traveler enters the airport premises,
the baggage status is changed to 'airport area,' and then to 'check-in
counter,' afer the baggage has been checked in. Other baggage status,
such as 'conveyor belt,' 'in-transit to airport,' 'loading oﬀ,' 'loading onboard,'
and 'onboard,' depicts the subsequent status at the outbound airport.
The status 'onboard' will be shown to travelers in the mobile app to enable
for improved baggage management between the traveler's origin and
destination. The baggage will be loaded oﬀ and moved to the conveyor
belt in various future states, such as when arriving at the destination. For
tourists to verify the status of their baggage, these two procedures are
provided in the smartphone app. The procedure depicted in the picture
with an RFID antenna symbol represents the region that will be tracked and
presented in the mobile app.
The Android Sofware Development Kit (SDK) and MIT App Inventor 2 (AI2)
are used to create the mobile app [42]. SDK is utilized because it facilitates
the building of applications for sofware packages, hardware platorms,
and computer systems, and it supports the Java and C++ coding platorms.
AI2 is a cloud-based application that runs in the Google Chrome browser
and includes the Adobe Flash Player. The App Inventor block editor was
used to create the baggage tracking, history log display, and login
elements of the mobile app. Figure 8 depicts the user interfaces built for
the mobile app's login and primary menu pages.
Users may log in using their e-mail address, barcode scanner, or Near Field
Communication (NFC) device. The main page connects to the Tracking,
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 283 of 346

History, Information, and Seting modules of the user interface. The mobile
app is meant to show the most recent state as well as the history of the
prior ﬁve statuses (Figure 9).
The connection between the Access database and the mobile app involves
the usage of Google's Fusion table. It stores the data using IoT technologies
before showing it on the mobile app. Examples of the Fusion tables are
provided in Figures 10 and 11. From the generation of the tag in the POS
through the updating of the tag and customer information to the following
statuses observed in the reader, the built IoTs are put to the test.
When the reading distance is larger than three meters and the tag moves
around the perimeter of the antenna, the performance of the reading
range has been evaluated. When compared to similar RFID performance
tests [12], the reading performance is adequate. A baggage safe protector
module in the mobile application has been built with login identiﬁcation by
password validation, QR code or NFC scan, taking security and privacy into
account. For security concerns, the information saved in the tag is
restricted to the name and cellphone number during the early
development stage.
In recent years, airlines and airports have conducted trials and
deployments of RFID on baggage handling. In 2005, Hong Kong, Milan
Malpensa, and Las Vegas McCarran airports were the ﬁrst to implement
RFID in airports [43]. The read rate of the new bar codes is over 97 percent,
compared to roughly 80 percent for traditional bar codes. Through more
precise tag reading and greater visibility across the whole baggage
operation, RFID is expected to cut mishandling by 20%. Other airlines and
airports have also conducted trials, such as British Airways at Heathrow,
Japan Airlines at Kansai International Airport and Hong Kong International
Airport, Kuala Lumpur International Airport, and Korean airports, among
others.
RFID's advantages in baggage handling include not just minimizing
mistreatment and improving visibility, but also improved monitoring and
lowering the usage of paper sticky baggage tags. Through partnership
between the airport, airline, and baggage maker, technology and research
for reusable baggage tags should advance for baggage monitoring
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 284 of 346

anytime, anyplace. The usage of a reusable baggage tag in conjunction
with an RFID tag implanted in the baggage might help to decrease the
annual waste of billions of adhesive baggage tags. This has a good
inﬂuence on the environment since it eliminates waste that is harmful to
the ecosystem.
The use of mobile tracking for baggage management connects the
passenger to the baggage position and ﬂight information. With baggage
monitoring, hotels participating in the project can improve their check-in
process and deliver beter customer service. Further development will
require integration throughout the systems, including the POS, DMS,
mobile applications, airport check-in counters, destination airports, and
hotels, with the created IT with DMS, fusion table, and mobile tracking
functions.
VI. Challenges
Various hurdles await the complete adoption of baggage monitoring from
retail to airport and then to hotel systems. Various counterparties in the
chain, including the baggage maker, retail store, airport, airline, hotel, and
customs, must communicate and collaborate. Platorms for data exchange,
system integration, and information ﬂow among diverse stakeholders, as
mentioned in previous sections, must be built.
Acceptance from all parties involved is another stumbling block to a
successful launch. In everyday operations, workers who are accustomed to
the existing practice rely on the Kanban method. The transition from paper
sticky baggage tags to digital information necessitates frontline worker
training and instruction.
When a reader detects the position of the baggage, it is able to track the
baggage's location but not its direction. The system might be blamed for a
lack of vector data. Detecting whether a person is coming or exiting an
airport when many signals are intercepted in the same location, for
example, might be contentious. The use of IoT baggage tracking may not
be enough to completely eradicate misplaced baggage.
Thef could still occur at the airport baggage pickup area Security would
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 285 of 346

Thef could still occur at the airport baggage pickup area. Security would
be critical to safeguard the growing amount of user data contained in the
tag, as well as the interfaces between the tag, readers, and IoT devices. The
RFID tag is permanently lodged in the baggage to prevent it from being
removed or the baggage ID from being changed. By implementing new
security measures, security might be improved further, such as a unique ID
number encoded in the tag which links to a protected and encrypted
database. Reader-tag authorization and shifing data into backend could
be implemented to enhance the security system.

is seen as critical to achieving greater real-world eﬃciency. According to
an IATA estimate [57], the top 80 airports must be equipped with RFID in

A Research Essay Concerning Electronic Baggage Tags
By: Edris Nazparvar
Technology should be used to aid us in our day-to-day life. This essay is a
collection of Articles concerning Electronic Baggage Tags, an electronic
device made for tracking and identifying Baggage. There are some key
questions concerning mentioned subject that this essay will try to provide
answers for them: What is an Electronic Baggage Tag? What are the
beneﬁts of using it instead of a traditional paper-based Baggage tag? What
can be the diﬃculties of using it? what are the technological
speciﬁcations?
I. Abstract
The aviation sector is one of the industries where Radio Frequency
Identiﬁcation (RFID) and the Internet of Things (IoT) have a lot of promise.
Mislaid baggage, lost baggage, and damage to valuables are the most
typical ﬂaws in the aviation industry's baggage handling. With over two
billion people traveling by air each year, the aviation industry is under
pressure to enhance baggage monitoring and minimize the use of one-
time sticky baggage paper tags.
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 302 of 346

RFID stands for radio frequency identiﬁcation and is a kind of automated
identiﬁcation. To cover all elements of airline baggage monitoring,
sortation, and reconciliation, several distinct types of RFID prototype
systems are currently being developed. RFID is not only a viable, unique,
and cost-eﬀective choice for everyday object identiﬁcation, but it is also
seen as a critical instrument for providing traceable visibility across the
aviation supply chain's many phases.
RFID scanners at check-out facilities allow for step-by-step baggage
tracking, preventing baggage loss. The baggage's real-time location is
recorded and saved in the cloud via IoT, and the travelers' unique ID may be
accessed anywhere and whenever they need it. At check-out counters, the
same ID can be used to collect the bag. The technology supplied saves
time, assures baggage security, and is cost-eﬀective, resulting in customer
happiness.
II. Introduction
In 2009, global airlines lost 25 million pieces of passenger baggage [1]. On
foreign ﬂights in 2011, 26 million pieces of checked baggage were missing
[2]. 25.8 million pieces of baggage were mishandled in 2012, with 85
percent being delayed and 15% being damaged or lost [3]. Some baggage
is located following track and trace within a few hours, while others are
declared permanently lost. Airports and airlines expend enormous energy
to seek down and return lost baggage to passengers, at tremendous
expense; Swedberg [6] estimated an annual cost of US$750 million to track
down and restore lost baggage to its owner through airline, airport, and
courier.
The IATA RP1740c protocol is the one that is used to recognize RFID
baggage tags. The RFID tags are used to enhance the ability for baggage
tracking, dispatching and conveyance to improve the management
eﬃciency and the users' satisfaction. The fundamental RFID system is
composed of three components: Transponder/Tag, Reader, Backend
Application. RFID technology has been investigated and deployed in a
number of airports across the world, including Heathrow Airport in London,
Kansai International Airport in Japan, and Hong Kong International Airport
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 303 of 346

Kansai International Airport in Japan, and Hong Kong International Airport
in Hong Kong.
III. Background
III.A: Standard
There are two important standards that will play signiﬁcant roles in the
supply chain: The ISO 18000 serial standard developed by the International
Standards Organization (ISO) and the EPC standard advanced by
EPCglobal. The EPC standard addresses air interfaces, the format for
product identiﬁcation data contained in RFID tags, middleware [74], and
databases that hold tag data. The ISO 18000-6C standard was met by the
EPC Class -1 Generation 2 UHF Speciﬁcation provided by EPCglobal. It
speciﬁes the speciﬁcations for a radio-frequency identiﬁcation system
operating in the 860MHz to 960MHz frequency band with passive-back
scater and Interrogator talk ﬁrst. This frequency range avoids regional
variances in UHF radio spectrum allotment for RFID, such as in North
America (902 to 928MHz), Europe (865.6 to 867.6MHz), and Japan (from
953 to 954MHz) [62].
An interrogator (also known as a reader) is in charge of reading data from a
tag by sending it a continuous-wave RF signal. The tag replies by modifying
its antenna's reﬂection coeﬃcient, backscatering an information signal to
the interrogator. The RF signal provides both information and working
energy to the tag, which is passive [48]. Because UHF works well in dry,
non-metallic setings, it is recommended that the aircraf baggage tag
operate in the UHF frequency range. The UHF RFID tag could be
reprogrammed and reused. By establishing self-service kiosks, operating
expenses might be minimized.
III.B: IATA
In terms of baggage handling regulations, the IATA, in collaboration with
airline companies, airports, and RFID vendors, addresses the open protocol
known as RP1740c [46][54]. It speciﬁes that the air interface used is ISO
18000-6C, and that the application protocol is ISO/IEC 15961 & 15962, which
describe data compression for storage and the instructions that may be
used to interact with the tag, respectively.
Table 1 (IATA Procedures): Covers IATA Resolution 740 (License Plate
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 304 of 346

Table 1 (IATA Procedures): Covers IATA Resolution 740 (License Plate
Number / LPN structure), IATA Recommended Practice RP1740c (ISO
18000-6C UHF RFID standards), and IATA Recommended Practice
RP1745 (Baggage Services Messages for structured DCS/BRS data
exchange).
III.C: Airports & Airlines
Many airlines and airports (Hong Kong International, Delta, McCarran,
British Airways/Heathrow, Singapore, Narita) have been working on
conversion from barcode to RFID. Adoption modes range from low-cost
tags ($0.25) indexing a single bag against a backend database to more
expensive tags with extended memory storing local baggage history.

IV.A: Design
Figure 0: Topology of the RFID-Based Traceable Air Baggage Handling
System (showing tag-sealed baggage strips, multi-directional
Intermec ﬁxed antennas, TCP/IP readers, Control Center servers, GPRS
SMS gateways, and distributed GIS monitors).
When passengers check in, the LPN, Carrier ID, Destination, Date,
Time, Passenger Name, Mobile, and CRC are writen to the tag's
memory block.

UHF RFID (862–900 MHz) tags interacting with Document
Management Systems (DMS) and mobile applications.

TagType , Address , BlockNo , DataLen , Data , CRC ).
IV.D: Implementation & Results
Figures 3 4 & 5: Deployed at Beijing Capital International Airport

Electrical and Hardware Safety Precaution
When building custom microelectronic hardware or interfacing with
governmental inspection equipment (such as TSA X-ray and EDS
systems), always ensure workbench power is isolated, use ESD-safe
grounding mats, and verify circuit isolation before soldering or logic-
board integration.
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 326 of 346

board integration.
To build the custom hardware electronics, conﬁgure the logic tables, and
interface the system with TSA inspection portals, follow the step-by-step
implementation guide below.
1
Construct the Hybrid Hardware Core
Prerequisite & PCB Design
Assemble the dual-mode smart tag hardware using a low-power
microcontroller (such as an ARM Cortex-M0+) integrated with an ams
AS3911 or equivalent UHF RFID frontend chip supporting ISO 18000-
6C (EPC Class-1 Gen 2) operating in the 860MHz–960MHz band.
Solder an onboard secondary UWB/BLE AoA chip (such as Nordic
nRF52840 paired with a Decawave DW1000 UWB module) for high-
precision spatial telemetry. Integrate a photovoltaic energy-
harvesting circuit (15–50 µW output) connected to a 3.3V micro-
regulator to power the active tracking states.
Veriﬁcation: Use a digital multimeter to verify that the regulated
output voltage at the microcontroller VDD pin maintains a steady 3.3V
under load, and check that the quiescent current draw does not
exceed 3.6 nW in sleep mode.
2
Program the Reader-to-Tag Communication Logic
Firmware Byte Packets
Program the reader ﬁrmware to execute the exact byte packet
structures for interrogating and writing to the tag memory blocks.
Implement the uncompressed command request format sent from
the reader to the tag:
Request Packet Structure: MsgLen (2 bytes) | Flags (2 bytes:
select/inventory/loop mode) | Command (2 bytes) | TagType (2
bytes: EPC C1 Gen2 / ISO 18000-C) | Address (2 bytes) |
https://gemini.google.com/share/ca13f3dfcc46?social_preview=false&skid=4a3889a0-ac70-4188-9918-d6221b3e82ae
10/19/26, 1:59 AM
Page 327 of 346

BlockNo (2 bytes) | DataLen (2 bytes) | Data (DataLen bytes)
| CRC (2 bytes: calculated CCITT-16).
Response Packet Structure: MsgLen (2 bytes) | RepCode (2
bytes) | TagType (2 bytes) | DataLen (2 bytes) | Data
(DataLen bytes) | CRC (2 bytes).
Veriﬁcation: Use an oscilloscope or logic analyzer connected to the
SPI data lines between the reader RF transceiver and baseband
processor to conﬁrm correct transmission and CRC checksum
validation of command packets.
3
Conﬁgure the Microsof Access & Fusion Tables Database Logic
Backend Mapping
Establish the relational database schema in Microsof Access
containing the 9 core tables linked via primary and foreign keys:
Customers, Tag Information, Retail shop for tag,
Flight information, Stocks, Baggage History, Baggage
location, Hotel, and Airline. Set up automated export triggers
to synchronize record transactions instantly with Google Fusion
Tables for cloud accessibility.
Veriﬁcation: Insert a mock test record into the Tag Information
table via a POS terminal entry and conﬁrm that the row correctly
propagates to the Google Fusion Table and reﬂects on the mobile
client interface.
4
Interface with TSA Screening & EBSP Hardware
Governmental Integration
Establish secure API endpoints and hardware communication
gateways to interface your baggage tracking database with the TSA
Electronic Baggage Screening Program (EBSP) automated X-ray and
Explosive Detection Systems (EDS). Map the bag's unique License
Plate Number (LPN) to TSA inspection clearance ﬂags so that

aviation industry (mishandled baggage costs airlines hundreds of millions

However, looking at it from a commercial, deployment, and adoption angle,
will it make money? And what are the success and failure rates? Here is
the unvarnished reality of the market mechanics:
1. Does the Idea Work?
Technically: Yes. Combining hybrid RAIN RFID (ISO 18000-6C) with
UWB/BLE spatial telemetry, power harvesting, and secure database
synchronization is entirely viable. Field trials at major hubs like Beijing
Capital International Airport (BCIA Terminal 2) have historically proven
that RFID architectures achieve high read-rate eﬃciencies (96.86%)
compared to traditional linear barcodes (75%–85%).
Strategically ("Trojan Horse" Approach): Yes, conditionally. Using a
consumer-facing e-commerce or duty-free app to build user demand
before atempting to integrate into regulatory or airport backends is a
proven path used by travel-tech startups. Airlines and airports are
notoriously slow to adopt custom standalone sofware unless it plugs
directly into existing Departure Control Systems (DCS) and Baggage
Reconciliation Systems (BRS) standards (such as IATA RP1740c and
newer messaging formats like BIX).
2. Will It Generate Money?
Revenue Potential: High, but with a grueling sales cycle.
B2C Revenue: Selling reusable smart electronic baggage tags
(EBTs) directly to frequent ﬂyers, combined with in-app duty-free
commissions and hotel logistics booking fees.
B2B / B2G Revenue: Licensing the sofware platorm or data-
broker endpoints to airports, ground handlers, and compliance
authorities (TSA/IATA) seeking automated four-point tracking
compliance (Resolution 753).

The Catch: Hardware margins on physical tags are thin due to
manufacturing costs, and airlines rarely pay third parties for
infrastructure upgrades directly. Proﬁtability depends entirely on
sofware-as-a-service (SaaS) licensing fees charged to airports or
retail merchants for the airside e-commerce and logistics hub.
3. Success Rate vs. Failure Rate (Estimated Projections)
In enterprise aviation and logistics tech, startup and novel ecosystem
initiatives face extreme friction. Based on historical industry deployments:
Estimated Failure Rate: ~75% to 85%
Why they fail:
1. The "Cold Start" and Interoperability Barrier: Airports and
ground handlers operate on razor-thin margins and strict
legacy protocols. Geting a proprietary app or portal
approved by airport authorities, security bodies (TSA), and
international coalitions (IATA) takes years of certiﬁcation.
2. Fragmentation and Resistance to Change: Airlines already
invest heavily in proprietary baggage tracking (e.g., RFID
baggage tags deployed by Delta, SITA data hubs, etc.).
Convincing them to replace or overlay their infrastructure
with a new vendor portal requires immense capital and
political lobbying.
3. Consumer Hardware Fatigue: Passengers are ofen reluctant
to purchase, charge, or manage a dedicated electronic tag
for luggage when smartphone-based tracking tiles (Apple
AirTags, Tile, SmartTags) already dominate the consumer
market at a fraction of the cost.
Estimated Success Rate: ~15% to 25%
How to land in the success bracket:
1. B2B2C Pivot: Instead of ﬁghting airlines directly, partner with
regional airports or duty-free retail conglomerates as an
exclusive airside commerce and logistics utility.

Summary Verdict
The engineering architecture is rock-solid, but pure-play commercial
success relies entirely on avoiding a direct turf war with legacy aviation
giants. If you market it strictly as a seamless integration layer that saves
airports money on mishandled baggage claims while capturing retail
commissions, it can become highly lucrative. If marketed as an aggressive
"replacement" for established global standards without airline backing, the
barrier to entry will cause it to stall during the pilot phase.

By positioning your sofware as an intelligent, non-invasive optimization

how modern aviation infrastructure actually evolves. Airports, airlines, and

Here is how your system works as an AI-driven optimization overlay:
1. The Integration Architecture (How It Connects Without
)

The API & Message Interoperability Layer: Your system plugs into
existing industry standards (such as IATA Recommended Practices
RP1740c, RP1745, and modern Baggage Information Exchange / BIX
data models). It acts as a middleware translation engine, intercepting
legacy Type B or XML messages from airline DCS and translating them
into real-time IoT events without altering underlying Programmable
Logic Controller (PLC) code.
The Uniﬁed Database & Cloud Sync: The Microsof Access and
Google Fusion Tables (or modern cloud data warehouses) operate as
an external metadata synchronization layer. They aggregate live scans
from hybrid RAIN RFID tags and UWB/BLE nodes, giving ground
handlers a uniﬁed dashboard while leaving the core airport conveyor
belts running uninterrupted.
2. What the AI Optimizes
Instead of breaking legacy workﬂows, your AI engine analyzes the
telemetry data ﬂowing through the legacy systems to solve critical
operational botlenecks:
Dynamic Routing & Transfer Optimization: Over 40% of baggage
mishandling occurs during tight ﬂight-to-ﬂight transfers. Machine
learning algorithms analyze live ﬂight delays, passenger walking
speeds, conveyor speeds, and transfer windows to dynamically re-
route or prioritize bag containers before a local delay turns into a
missed connection.
Predictive Congestion & Botleneck Mitigation: AI models predict
where carousel crowding or sorting blockages will occur downstream,
enabling ground handlers to proactively divert luggage streams.
Automated Exception Recovery: When a barcode is smudged or
unreadable, computer vision and RFID data-blending algorithms
instantly recover the bag's identity using historical trajectory
matching, drastically reducing manual "recode station" delays.

1. Does This Model Work? (The IP & SaaS Blueprint)
Yes, it is a proven playbook used by tech patent holders. Companies
like Qualcomm, InterDigital, and Sisvel license core communications,
IoT, and standard-essential patents (SEPs) to hardware manufacturers
without building phones or chips themselves.
The Mechanics:
1. Patent Licensing / Hardware Royalties: You ﬁle and hold the core
utility and sofware patents covering the hybrid RAIN
RFID/UWB/BLE tag architecture, cryptographic sync protocols,
and AI optimization algorithms. Any hardware manufacturer (tag
or reader makers) wishing to build compatible equipment must
license your patent portolio and pay a per-unit running royalty
(e.g., $0.05 to $0.20 per certiﬁed smart tag or reader sold).
2. The "Free" Government/Airline App (The Ultimate Trojan Horse):
By oﬀering the regulatory compliance portal (TSA/IATA Resolution
753 audit tools) completely free to government bodies and
airports, you instantly eliminate political and bureaucratic
resistance. They adopt it because it costs them nothing and
solves their compliance headaches.

infrastructure is ﬂooded with users via the free government and
consumer utility apps, you monetize through high-margin

Frequent Flyer / Premium Tier: Advanced predictive travel
alerts, automated insurance claims, and concierge features.
